- **The `reference` loader makes a load one turn, or two where a file needs more than one read.** A run that holds the loader's text word for word from an earlier request in the run knows the paths it names, so it makes a request's `Skill` call and the reads the text has it make in one turn — a file's line count (`wc -l` where the run has a shell) where a whole read of it needs its length, the entry-point rules where the request needs them, and each file's first read, with the Read tool, never `cat` — and any further ranges together in one more. No rule of the text is skipped for a read made before it arrives: once it arrives the run follows it as written and makes any different read it names, since a read the text does not call for serves no request, and a request it says fails fails, whatever was read. In the live runs a load took two to seven turns — the `Skill` call alone, then `wc -l`, then one read a turn — about $0.5–1.7 a run; the paragraph adds about 0.8k characters to every request.
- **`ard-resolution`'s gate reads `require-on-main`'s own file.** It executed `require-on-main` through a path to the whole of `phase-handoff`, so a run whose ARD resolved — `/dev-workflows:design`, `/dev-workflows:implement`, `/dev-workflows:ready`, `/dev-workflows:conformance`, `/product-workflows:create-ard`, `/product-workflows:specify` and `/product-workflows:epics` — read all of it in the phase that resolves the ARD, as under 1.44.0; it now requests the entry point, `args: "phase-handoff require-on-main"`, which reads the file, or nothing where the phase has read it already, and takes `<default-ref>` from `specs-repo-git` §3.2, which the run's preflight has read. `escalation-rules`' rule putting the review verdict into a specs-repository handoff's pull request names that handoff in words, where it cited `phase-handoff` §2, which a run loading the review or version-line part mid-run would have read whole.
- **The references page** says that `require-on-main` reads its own file and which citations read it with the request, and that every command presenting a choice list loads `escalation-rules core` before its first question; the workflow page's `reference-parts/` node names that file.

### Fixed
- **`grounding-format` §2.3 keeps the operator's model and machine out of a grounding file**: neither a finding's free text nor the prose around the blocks carries the model a run, a pass or a tool of the operator's ran on, by its id or its family's name — the tier a finding was ground or verified at included — or a directory of the operator's machine, recognised by its value and never by its shape — the checkout a repository was read in, the specs repository's directory, the documentation root the run resolved, or a scratch or home directory. A path or a route the customer's own code, configuration or documents name stays, quoted as they give it, whatever its shape — save one that is, or lies under, this machine's own checkout, specs or documentation path, to the letter, which `/product-workflows:prd-ground` replaces. A model id or a model family's name that the customer's own requirement, code, configuration or documents name stays too, quoted as given — in a finding's `claim`, beside the requirement id or the path it comes from, or as what a command searches for — where `product-workflows:bundle-packaging` §6.3 puts it to the operator's ruling rather than stopping the package on it. The operator's model and such a directory describe the operator's environment, and the customer's reviewer can do nothing with either — a command run against a repository is written `git -C "<repo>"`, as the three pin commands are, and the tier a corpus was ground at is reported in `/product-workflows:prd-ground`'s Final report. A run had written `Ground tier: claude-opus-5-5` into a `code-grounding.md` that goes to the customer as written, and kept a verifier note naming the operator's checkout. The section's list now names the prose around the blocks beside a finding's fields, which `/product-workflows:prd-ground` already held to it, and the references page names what the section keeps out.
- **`grounding-format` §3 says what a `CONFIRMED` on a claim that restricts a value has to show**, in a subsection of its own, *A claim that restricts a value*. A claim restricting a value — to a range, a set or a format, to one per something, to never changing once written — holds only where the restriction holds on every path that writes the value, so a `CONFIRMED` names each writer of it the repository holds — every way the record is created, every way the value is set or changed later, every input it is taken from, a request that leaves it out and the default it is then stored with among them, and any other writer — and shows the restriction applied on it. A writer the restriction is not shown to reach makes the verdict `AMENDED`, or `REWRITTEN` where the premise is materially wrong, naming that writer and what it can store, and where the repository holds the client that sends the request the finding says whether it can send it without the value. Listing the writers is evidence and owes no control; a sentence saying no other writer exists asserts an absence, which §2.2 governs as before, and so does the `AMENDED`'s statement that a writer is not reached, which owes §2.2's control like any absence read off a path. A run had confirmed that a value always lies within a range because a setter checks it, while a request that leaves the value out bypasses the setter and stores the type's default — and its blind re-derivation, having read the same line, confirmed it too. The references page names what such a `CONFIRMED` has to show.
- **`grounding-format` §2.2 owes a control on an absence however it was reached and wherever the finding states it.** An absence read off a code path in full — a function, a handler, a request's route through the code, with no step that does the thing — rests on that reading as an empty grep rests on the grep, and its control is the same reading pointed at a path of the same kind known to do it; and an absence stated in the correction an `AMENDED` or `REWRITTEN` finding makes owes one as one stated in its verdict does. The section opened by saying an absence claim rests on a search returning nothing, and a run's grounder and its blind re-derivation each stated an absence read off a method with no control; the comparison then forced a `contradict` on the missing control and the finding took a retry. The `control` field's row, `evidence`'s never-blank paragraph, *Same method* and the closed-set rule name a reading beside a search, and so does §8's sentence on a failed or missing control: two searches or readings sharing one blind spot.
- **`phase-handoff` §5 rule 2 says where a consumer runs the gate.** It said every consumer executes `require-on-main` in its Phase 0, which is false for four: `/dev-workflows:ready` runs it in Phase 1, `/product-workflows:epics` in Phase 2.6, `/product-workflows:specify` against an Epic its Step A picks, and `/product-workflows:brd-proposal` in Phase 4, each at the step that reaches the artifact it gates; and `/product-workflows:brd-reconcile`'s cross-BRD write guard runs it against another BRD's artifact, as §3.7 says. §3's opening said the same, and now says it runs in most callers' Phase 0 and in every caller after `specs-preflight`.
- **`session-hygiene` §1: a resume pointer keeps every item the one it overwrites carried until it is settled.** A run now reads the pointer it is about to replace — one per folder, written by every Epic's and every role's runs — and its *Carry-forward decisions* line holds, beside what the next phase needs, each item the old pointer carried that is not settled: each of its carry-forward items, and each open item of its *Next step* the new *Next step* does not name, one line each. *Settled* has one test, stated once in §1, cited by the template line and restated on both docs pages: this run settled the item, or the check for what the item waits on — a read that fetches and writes nothing — shows every branch, command, question or path it names settled; an item that waits on more than one of these — a push and then its pull request's merge, say — is settled only once each of its checks shows it settled, and one naming a pull request waits on that pull request's merge. An item waiting on a merge, of a branch or of the pull request it names, is settled by the merged check alone, never by a push: in the specs repository by `specs-repo-git` §3.5's `branch-merged`, which sees a merge, a rebase and a squash, asked of the ref `phase-handoff` §2.2 rule 3 resolves — `refs/remotes/origin/<name>` where it exists, else `refs/heads/<name>` — or by this run's preflight having found it merged (B2), as its printed block says or, for `/dev-workflows:implement`, as its run-state records; in a code repository by ancestry in the refs the run holds, or by `implementation-format` §5's merged test where the run applied it. An item asking for a push is settled once the branch is pushed; one asking for a follow-up, once its line is ticked; one asking for a command to run, once a record that command writes as the item asks it to run — in the item's own mode and role, and in or for the repository the item names where it names one — is later than the item's waiting datetime (the datetime it waits from: the one its line ends with as `(waiting since <ISO datetime>)`, or, for an item of the old pointer's *Next step* or one whose line ends with none — an older pointer's, say — the old pointer's *Last completed* datetime): in the folder the item names, an `implementation.md` block, a `_conformance.md` entry or a `_security.md` block whose `kind` and role are the item's and whose `repo` is the item's where it names one; for `/product-workflows:prd-ground`, a run's dated heading in `grounding/baselines.md` or `grounding/design-grounding.md`, files only that command writes a heading into — `/product-workflows:update-prd`, `/product-workflows:create-ard` and `/product-workflows:specify` write only `consumed_by`, inside a finding's block, never a heading — a `baselines.md` one, which every run but `--no-code` writes, for an item asking for a run that grounds code, and a `design-grounding.md` one, which every run writes, for an item asking for `--no-code`, neither settling an item asking for `--rebaseline`, a run they do not tell from another, nor, while `design-grounding.md`'s `## Frame sets covered` section, which each run rewrites, records a set `skipped: --no-design`, one that does not ask for `--no-design`; for `/dev-workflows:ready`, `_readiness.md`'s heading, `# Readiness check — <run timestamp, ISO 8601 UTC>`, which only that command writes, overwriting the file on each run; and, for `/security-workflows:scan`, a run record under `security/<repo-slug>/runs/`, written where the run changed the register, whose `kind` and `role` are those `security-register-format` §2 gives the run the item asks for — `kind` `verify` for `--verify-fixes`, `triage` for `--triage`, `import` for `--import` and `scan` for a scan, and `role` `dev` for a key run without `--triage` or `--verify-fixes` and `sec` otherwise — so no run of another mode or role settles it, with, on a repository path, `<repo-slug>` the register's slug the item names beside the path, kept with it by every run that carries it, read from the item and never derived from the path on a machine where the path is not that repository, and, on a key, a record whose `unit` is that key, in the register of the repository the item names or, where it names none, in any — the only record a `--triage` on a key leaves, since a run appends to `_security.md` only where it recorded a scan — a record that holds no mode or role of its own being of the mode and role its command and its folder fix, and each record dated by the datetime it records, `_readiness.md`'s compared with the waiting datetime directly — a waiting datetime written with no UTC offset, an older pointer's (the template's *Last completed* now carries its offset), read as UTC, and one with no time as the end of its UTC day, either reading erring to carrying wherever it is no earlier than the pointer was written — save that a block of an append-only record — `implementation.md`'s or `_security.md`'s, or a run's in `grounding/baselines.md` or `grounding/design-grounding.md` — records a day alone, and is dated by its heading's own day first (one whose day is earlier than the waiting datetime's day, in UTC, is never later, and one whose day is later always is, whatever commit carried it; only a block of that same day is dated by the commit that added its heading line, the commit `git -C "$SPECS_PATH" blame -L <n>,<n> --porcelain -- <file>` names for heading line `<n>`, at that commit's `author-time`, which a rebase keeps where it moves the committer time, never the file's last commit, which a later block of another repository, mode or role moves too, and a same-day block whose heading line blame names no commit for, or the all-zero commit, dates nothing — one in a file the branch the checkout stands on does not track, one not yet committed, or, in `specs-repo-git` §8's session-branch mode, one committed on the session branch and not yet on the branch the checkout stands on), a `_conformance.md` is dated as a block of an append-only record is, its `Run:` line standing for the heading line (by that line's day first and, on that same day, by the commit blame names for it), and a same-day block or `Run:` line whose blamed commit's `author-time` falls on a later UTC day than the line's own day dates nothing either, since a run commits the record it writes on the day it writes it, so a later day's commit is a re-write or a late one — a squash, a later run's flush — and a run that spans midnight UTC, committing its record on the next day, or one whose heading or `Run:` line carries a local date behind UTC, dates nothing by the same rule, which errs to carrying, the command check settling nothing wherever it cannot tell who wrote what it reads, or when, so the item is carried, never dropped; a decision, a fact or an open question, once a deliverable the next phase reads at the folder's top level records it — `prd.md`, `ard.md`, `epic.md`, `specification.md`, `design.md`, a `decisions.md` register or a `_*.md` record, never the pointer itself or a session file — the template's own *NOT already in the artifact* test applied to each of those; and one asking for a path inside a repository the item names with it to be removed, or to be written or committed, once the path is absent from, or present at, that repository's default ref — in the specs repository the `<default-ref>` the run's preflight resolved, in a code repository its default branch's tip in the refs the run holds — the same answer on every machine; a path an item names for any other reason — a command's argument (the `<repo-path>` of `/security-workflows:scan`'s Security Engineer offers among them), the deliverable a merge carries, a file a decision is about — is settled with its item, by the check for what the item waits on, and an item naming a worktree that `/security-workflows:scan` 0.2.0 or older failed to remove, the line that version's resume-pointer step wrote into *Carry-forward decisions*, is settled at once, as one the pointer never carries. A settled item is dropped, and a line the settling run kept to say so is dropped by the next run; an item not settled — by this run or by the check for what it waits on — is carried, so an item settled outside any run — a pull request merged in the forge, a branch pushed by hand — leaves the pointer once a run can see it. An item that waits on a pull request names its branch and its repository, so a later run can make the merged check, and an item that asks for a path to be removed, written or committed names the repository it lies in. An item that asks for a command to run is carried with its line ending `(waiting since <ISO datetime>)`, its waiting datetime copied as the old pointer writes it, and every run that carries the item keeps that datetime, never replacing it with the run's own *Last completed* datetime, so a record that reaches the checkout only after a later run wrote its pointer — pushed from another clone while a run that carried the item was under way, held on another session branch until its pull request merged, or kept local by a rejected push until it was pushed — still settles the item; the datetime is all the marker adds to the item's one line, naming no path and nothing the redaction rule removes. A path outside every repository the pointer's readers share — a scratch worktree, a temporary directory — is never written into the pointer, since the pointer is shared while such a path belongs to one machine, so its absence on one cannot be told from its removal on another, and a run that leaves one names it in its final report and its feedback instead. These stay longer than they need, and §1 lists them: a code repository's branch merged by a squash or a rebase, until a run applies `implementation-format` §5's merged test to it — a run of the Epic the branch belongs to, or `/dev-workflows:conformance <PRD>`; a branch or a path in a repository the run holds no ref for, or only refs older than the merge, the removal or the write — a code repository the run did not open or did not fetch, or a branch never fetched here — until a run holding current refs settles it; a specs-repository branch with neither its local nor its `origin/` ref left — deleted and pruned before any run that wrote a pointer here saw it merged — which, without a fetch, cannot be told from a branch never fetched here, so it stays carried; an item asking for `/security-workflows:scan` whose run changed nothing in the register, and so wrote no run record, or whose scan a run of another mode recorded — an `--import` of its report, say — until a run of the item's own mode and role records and so settles it, for good where none can, as no change scan records once `unit-branch` reads its Epic's branch merged with no work after it; an item only a block of `implementation.md` or `_security.md` of its waiting datetime's own day can settle, in `specs-repo-git` §8's session-branch mode, until the session branch's pull request merges — by a merge or a rebase, or by a squash on the block's own day — and the block reaches the branch the checkout stands on; an item only a day-dated block or `Run:` line can settle whose commit falls on a later UTC day than the line's own day — a pull request a squash merged on a later day, a later run's flush, a run past midnight UTC, a heading written in a local date behind UTC — until a record of a later day settles it, or a run of the item's command writing this pointer settles it itself, and for good where that command writes another folder's pointer and nothing runs it again: `/product-workflows:prd-ground <CHILD-KEY>` in a root BRD's pointer after a *Ground now* whose pull request a squash merged on a later day; an item asking for a command that leaves none of the records this check reads — one whose only record is its deliverable, a whole file the check does not read: most name no date of their own, and other commands commit into several — or asking for one in a mode those records do not tell — `/product-workflows:prd-ground`'s `--rebaseline`, or a run of it that grounds design while `design-grounding.md`'s `## Frame sets covered` records a set `skipped: --no-design` — until a run of that command writing this pointer settles it itself, so one whose command ran on another clone without this pointer in front of it stays until then, and for good where that command writes another folder's pointer or none: `/product-workflows:brd-proposal` in a slice's pointer and `/product-workflows:prd-proposal` in its root's or a sibling's, whose `priced-against` record names each input's content and no time, and `/workflows-core:frames`, which writes none; an item asking for `/security-workflows:scan` on a repository path with no register slug beside it — a Security Engineer offer an older `/security-workflows:scan` wrote, say — until a run on a machine where that path is that repository, a repository with an `origin` that `git -C <path> rev-parse --show-toplevel` names, settles it by the command check, with the slug it derives there as `/security-workflows:scan` does (`workflows-core`'s `components` §1), every other run carrying it; and an item an older pointer wrote naming a pull request by its number alone, until a run settles it. One can leave sooner than it should: blame names the commit that put a line on the branch the checkout stands on, so a block or a `_conformance.md` of an item's waiting datetime's own day that a later commit of that same day carried — a squash merge, a hand commit of a handoff its run declined, a later run's flush — is dated by that commit and can settle the item though it was written before that datetime; and so can a block whose heading, or a `_conformance.md` whose `Run:` line, carries a local date ahead of UTC, written between local midnight and UTC midnight: its day is later than the UTC day it was written on, so the day-first rule reads it as later than a waiting datetime of that UTC day that came after it; and an item whose waiting datetime an older pointer wrote in a local time behind UTC with no offset, or as a date alone written behind UTC after UTC midnight, can leave sooner too, since that datetime is read as UTC, or as the end of its UTC day, and so as earlier than it was. The template's "decisions the next phase needs" had let runs drop another lane's pending work: in lean context D's live runs, at least six pointer writes across four runs lost a live item — a PM's pending `/update-prd`, a PA's ARD refine, an Epic's conformance re-run, an unpushed branch and a merge order among them. The pointer is still overwritten, never appended to. The two resume-and-checkpoints pages say so, and `next-phase-offer`'s *Dev — build, verify & deliver* lane writes the register, `security/<repo-slug>/`, beside both Security Engineer offers, as `/security-workflows:scan` does.
- **`next-phase-offer` names the remaining Epics.** A new rule, *The remaining Epics are named*, in a helper part only the `dev` lane loads, so the other lanes, none of which says "the remaining Epics", read nothing more: where a lane line says "the remaining Epics", the offer lists each other Epic folder under the PRD by its key — the folder listing is the set — with what the run can see of it (`no implementation record`, `implemented — not merged` where it ran `implementation-format` §5's merged test, else `implemented — merge not checked`; one shown merged is left out) and its next step — `/dev-workflows:implement <EPIC>` for one with no record, and for one implemented the Dev lane's own steps before its merge, `/dev-workflows:conformance <EPIC>` and then `/security-workflows:scan <EPIC>`, each beside the record its folder holds, then its review and merge — and orders them with this Epic's own pull request by the `### Landing order` the PRD folder's `ard.md` writes, each placed by its `epic.md`'s `target:`; where a remaining Epic's target lands first, the offer says this Epic's pull request merges after it, and where the ARD records no order, the offer asserts none. Where the run's unit is the PRD folder itself, every Epic under it is a remaining one. The `/dev-workflows:implement`, `/dev-workflows:conformance <EPIC>` and `/security-workflows:scan <EPIC>` lane lines order the merges by it. A scan run's next step had told the Dev to merge the storefront Epic's pull request and then finish "the remaining Epics", while the ratings Epic it consumes, first in the ARD's landing order, was unmerged; and an `/implement` run printed a raw `<SIBLING-EPIC>` placeholder.
- **`specs-repo-git` §4 step 7: nothing after a report printed before the tail restates it.** Where the run printed its report before `commit-artifacts`, it prints from then on only what the tail's own steps print — the maintenance, feedback, follow-up and cost lines, any question one of them asks, and the `Specs repo:` line with its notice and appends — and any line the command names to follow it, never a recap of the report or one of its lines again, its `Code repo:` or `Phase handoff:` line among them; a cede-control run's hand-off ends the span. §6 says its terminal block holds nothing else. An `/implement` run's closing recap, written after the tail, claimed four files matched a reviewed commit where the report correctly said three, and printed a second `Code repo:` line, and a scan run's recap claimed a coverage gap it could not know.

### Added — the repository's gates (not shipped in any plugin)
- **`scripts/reference-parts.py` generates and gates an entry-point file.** A `<!-- reference-entry-files` declaration after an unsplit reference's title names an entry point and the sections it holds whole or in part, and `<!-- entry: … -->` regions select its lines. `generate` writes the file, and `check-parts` fails it where it is stale or missing, where a line of a section held whole lies outside the entry's regions, where region text lies in a section the entry does not declare, where a phrase of a section held in part is not in the region text — a phrase naming a numbered rule, `rule <N>`, only where a region there holds the rule's own item, `N.`, never a mention of it in the section's opening line — and on a redundant or malformed declaration; a positional pointer in a region prints as a `FLAG`. `check-citations` fails where the loader's list of these entry points differs from the declaration, or its entry-point rules' sentence naming what the file holds does; where the list's paragraph lacks, word for word, the sentence giving each file's path or the one that has a request read that rules file, or the file is missing; and where the loader's own text names what the file holds, which belongs to the rules file alone.
- **`check-citations` fails a citation the file does not serve**: in a `##` section requesting `phase-handoff require-on-main`, a sentence citing a section the file does not hold — or holds in part, for other text — or citing the reference by no section, with no whole load of it at or before the sentence; the same sentence in a later section, before the command's first load of the whole reference, unless its own sentence makes that load; and a citation of the reference before the first such section in its command, unless its own sentence loads the whole reference. Each would load the whole reference beside the file. It fails too where a declared entry point is requested by no `##` section of any file it scans, whose check would then judge nothing.
- **`check-questions`**, a subcommand of its own: a command whose text holds a `choices:` array, presents `phase-handoff` §4.3's consent array, or states a prompt of 2–4 options it builds at run time — a line holding `2–4 options` that names no `` `choices` `` array, whose rule such a line states instead — fails unless a **Load the question rules.** step that loads `escalation-rules core` starts on an earlier line than its first question. It fails closed on a scan that finds no asking command, and CI runs it after `check-citations`.
- **`.claude/rules/gates-reference-parts.md`** describes the entry-point file and both checks, with what they cannot see; `docs/maintainers/rationale.md` § reference-parts records why `require-on-main` reads a file of its own rather than `phase-handoff` being split, and why the question rules load as a step; `CLAUDE.md` names the entry-point file and the question-rules step; `.claude/rules/workflows-core.md` and `.claude/rules/workflows-core-git.md` say what the loader reads for `require-on-main`, and where its rules for that file are.
- **`scripts/check-docs.sh`'s check-16 header** counts this tree's loader invocations again, from its own scan; only the comment's figures change.

## [1.44.0] — 2026-10-22

**Update `dev-workflows` to 4.30.0, `product-workflows` to 3.35.0, `docs-workflows` to 1.17.0 and `security-workflows` to 0.2.0 with this release**: each of them now asks the `reference` loader for a part of `escalation-rules`, `finding-triage`, `next-phase-offer` or `session-hygiene`, which only this release's loader serves — an older one reads a part's name as an entry point of the whole reference, which no heading names as one, and may run a section whose heading merely contains that word. An older dependent beside this release requests no part, and the loader reads each reference whole for it as before, with one exception: `/product-workflows:promote-decisions` before 3.35.0 loads an `architecture-promotion` section by its name, and this loader fails a request whose second argument names none of the entry points of a reference that does not load by part, and the run stops as its command stops.

A run reads less of the shared references, with every rule still in front of it where it applies: a reference already in the conversation word for word is not read again — save to execute an entry point in a later phase than the one that read it — and a caller of one of the four rulebooks reads the part its step needs rather than the whole file.

### Added
- **The `reference` loader reuses a read made in the same run, within one bound.** A run works from the reference's own words, never from memory or a summary. The text a request needs is the whole file on a reference that does not load by part, whichever entry point the request names, and on any reference a request with no second argument; a read short of every line — a section, a range, a search — covers no such request, while consecutive ranges from the first line to the last, read where one read cannot hold the file, are one read of every line. Where the text a request needs — the whole file, or the part asked for — is already in the conversation word for word, from a read made in this run, the run works from that text and reads nothing, save that it executes an entry point only from a read made in the current phase: the `##` section of the command now running — a `## Phase`, a `## Step` or any other, the text before its first `##` heading counting as one — or an agent's whole dispatch. To look up a rule, any read of the run serves. The run reads the file whenever those words are not in front of it, whatever removed them — it was never read, only a different part of it was read, or an earlier read has since been replaced by a summary or cleared, a summary of a reference never being its text — and wherever it cannot tell. A read of the whole file covers every entry point in it, within that bound. Whether the text was read now or is already in the conversation, it is the single source of truth for the step, an entry point is executed inline and nothing is paraphrased or summarised; the loader no longer says "never cache". So, while the earlier read is still in the conversation, a producer command that loads `phase-handoff` (136.5k characters) whole for its consent choice no longer reads it a second time for `handoff-to-main` in the same phase, and `/product-workflows:specify` no longer reads `escalation-rules` up to four times.
- **The `model-routing` skill reuses its read the same way.** Its request is always the whole of `model-routing/classification`, which a read short of every line does not cover, consecutive ranges from its first line to its last being one read of every line. Classifying executes that file, so a run classifies from a word-for-word read of it already in the conversation only where that read was made in the current phase, as the loader defines one, and reads the file otherwise; to look up one of its rules, any read of the run serves. It reads the file whenever those words are not in front of it, whatever removed them, and wherever it cannot tell. The skill now names where the rules are rather than ordering a read ahead of that condition.
- **Four rulebooks load by part.** `escalation-rules`, `finding-triage`, `next-phase-offer` and `session-hygiene` each declare their parts after their title and mark which part owns each line. Each stays one source file, the only one anyone edits, and still holds every rule it held. Each has an always part, `core`, that comes with every part. The parts a caller can request:
  - **`escalation-rules`** — `core`: §0 with *A selection carries no text*, *Choice lists are presented verbatim*, *The `(Recommended)` marker is unconditional* with *When no option is safe to recommend*, and *When a choice list fires*. `pickers`: *Closed-vocabulary pickers must normalise the free-text answer*. `stops`: *key dir not found* and *Required path environment variable unset*. `scan`: repository resolution, stashing and a dirty tree, a refresh that is blocked and a read-only mount. `branch-prefix`: *Branch prefix undetected*. `version-line`: *A recorded verdict names the version it was taken against*. `review`: the review gate's rules from the version line to the review verdict, bringing `version-line` with it, about 31.6k characters. It brings no `finding-triage` text: a command that triages its review loads `finding-triage`'s `re-review` at its own triage step, before it reaches these rules, and the commands that fix inline and do not triage their re-review — `/product-workflows:create-prd`, `/product-workflows:update-prd`, `/product-workflows:create-ard`, `/product-workflows:specify` and `/dev-workflows:design` — settle it by these rules alone.
  - **`finding-triage`** — `core`: the station rule, that the orchestrator triages and never the fixer. `patch-gate`, for the fixers. `verify`, the verification step, for `/dev-workflows:conformance`. `no-findings`, for a review that returned none. `triage`: when triage runs, § The step, § When triage empties the survivor set, the patch gate and § Reporting. `re-review`: `triage` and § On re-review.
  - **`next-phase-offer`** — `core`: what the reference is for. `contract`: the seven offer rules, the merge-clause rule and its resolution table, the universal minimum, the overflow rule and the session-hygiene co-firing note. One part per lane of the routing graph — `pm`, `brd`, `proposals`, `pa`, `pe` and `dev` — each bringing `contract`, and, where its commands can offer `/product-workflows:epics`, `epics-precondition`. `epics-precondition` alone, for the Phase 0 redirect of `/dev-workflows:design` and `/dev-workflows:implement`. `overflow`, for a mid-run picker. `not-pipeline`, for the documentation commands that print a universal-minimum `### Next step`. The maintainer material is in no part a run requests.
  - **`session-hygiene`** — `core`: the guidance-only rule and §4's definition of `<KEY>` and `<role>`, which the resume pointer's title and suggested session name use. `resume-pointer`: §1. `guidance`: §2 to §5 and *Surface*.
- **`reference-parts/`.** Each requestable part is assembled into a generated file, `reference-parts/<name>/<part>.md`, holding the always part, the part, the parts it needs and the reference's rule index, which names the part that holds each rule; `reference-parts/<name>/index.md` holds the index alone. Each generated file opens by naming its source and saying it is never edited by hand, and each part file's title is followed by a line naming every part the file holds — "This file holds parts `core`, `version-line` and `review`." — so a run holding it can tell which parts it already has.
- **The loader serves a part.** On one of the four, a second argument names a part — `args: "session-hygiene resume-pointer"` reads `reference-parts/session-hygiene/resume-pointer.md` — and `args: "<name> index"` reads the index; with no second argument the whole reference is read, as for every other. A read of the whole reference covers each of its parts, save what a part bundles from another reference, and a part already read covers itself and every part the line under its title names. A second argument that names no part of one of the four is no stop: the loader reports it, reads that reference's rule index, and then reads the part the index names for the rule the step applies — or the whole reference, where the index names none — never reading anything else in its place and never applying a rule it has not read. On any other reference a second argument names an entry point — the first word of a code span in one of that reference's headings, such as `resolve-address` in "### Entry point: `resolve-address <ARG> [<KIND>]`" — and one that names none of them fails the request: the loader reports the argument that named nothing, and the run stops as its command, or its agent, stops. The loader's description says it reads a part.
- **`finding-triage` § A review that returned no findings**, which opens part `no-findings`: every such review takes steps 3 to 5 of § The step, whose step 5 rules on a `### Declined to judge` list, and § Reporting; a `PASS`, or a re-review whose verdict is not `BLOCK`, takes nothing else; a first review whose verdict is not `PASS` also takes § When triage empties the survivor set, through its first settle prompt, so a verdict nothing supports never passes silently; and a re-review that is `BLOCK` also takes § On re-review rules 2 and 3. The two last name the part to load, unless it is already in the conversation word for word: `triage`, or `re-review`, which holds it, for the first, and `re-review`, which holds `triage` too, for the second.
- **The references page** describes the references that load by part, how a caller names a part, the `index` request and the generated files, what the loader does with a second argument that names no part, and, for both skills, the text a request needs, when a read already in the conversation serves — to execute an entry point or classify, only one made in the current phase — and when the file is read again, and the stop on a second argument that names no entry point; the workflow page draws `reference-parts/` between the references and the loader.

### Changed
- **What moved so that a part holds the rules it needs, every rule kept:**
  - `escalation-rules`' paragraph on what *Specify a different absolute path for this repo* asks next, which every *Repo unresolved (zero matches)* variant and *Repo missing (after resolution)* share, sat inside *Repo unresolved (zero matches) — /epics*; it opens a section of its own, *Repo resolution — specifying a different absolute path*, ahead of the variants.
  - *A verdict that is not `BLOCK` spends neither half of the cap* is stated as the rule of *Edits after a verdict that is not BLOCK — the unspent re-review*, ahead of the live `/create-prd` run it came from, which follows as its own paragraph.
  - The version line's close names *Findings a review leaves open — the offer to apply them* and *Edits after a verdict that is not BLOCK — the unspent re-review*, the two offers that can follow a review, each naming the commands that make it, where it pointed at "the next section" and "the section after the next".
  - `next-phase-offer` says in its contract when `<merge-clause>` is not attached — an offer that names no downstream gate its run feeds, as `/product-workflows:brd-proposal`'s, `/security-workflows:scan`'s and `/workflows-core:frames`' do — and `/product-workflows:create-ard`'s `/product-workflows:harvest-decisions` offer, which carries it all the same. These sentences stood in the paragraph on where the rule applies, which now sits in no part a run requests.
  - `finding-triage` § When triage empties the survivor set says that a caller whose own rule keeps a first review's `BLOCKER` from the fixer, because its fix lies in another code repository, counts that review as one that stayed blocked and takes its stop or escalation over that `BLOCKER`, as **Keep the verdict** does — § On re-review rule 2's clause, now also where a first review reads it — and its step 2 names **Keep the verdict** as the stop rule 2 names.
  - A positional pointer that a part would carry away from its target — "above", "below", "this file", "the next section" — names its target instead, across the four.
- **`workflows-core`'s own citations of the four name the rule they apply**: `branch-naming` §2.5's canonical wording is `workflows-core:escalation-rules`' *Branch prefix undetected*, where it named a bare file; `model-routing/classification` §6 cites `finding-triage` § The step; `escalation-rules` cites `next-phase-offer`'s *overflow rule* and *Resolving this placeholder is not a rewording*, and its review rules cite `finding-triage` § On re-review, § The step and § When triage empties the survivor set where they named the bare file — each where the command triages, as its sentence says, and the rule for a re-review that stays `BLOCK` in the commands that fix inline says that the five of them that do not triage their re-review take it on the `BLOCK` verdict itself; `next-phase-offer` cites `session-hygiene` §2; `/workflows-core:frames` cites the *merge-clause rule*, and `/workflows-core:statusline` *rule 6*.
- **`doc-fixer` reads `finding-triage`'s `patch-gate` part**, about 3.5k characters, by its path, where it read the whole 21.4k-character reference, and its *survivor* sentence no longer cites the reference for what its caller did.
- **`/workflows-core:frames`' printed context-hygiene line** cites `session-hygiene` §5, the section it means.
- **`dependencies`** says the loader's optional second argument names an entry point or, on a reference that loads by part, the part to read.

### Fixed
- **`finding-triage`'s summary of a re-review** in § When triage empties the survivor set says no survivor is handed to a fixer *save one the user applies at `escalation-rules`' offer to apply what a review leaves open*, as § On re-review rule 1 and § Reporting already did; § Reporting names that offer where it said "that offer".
- **`finding-triage` continues a `PASS WITH RECOMMENDATIONS` without a fixer only where its survivors hold none the fixer takes.** It read "with no survivor its fixer takes", which also covered a set triage emptied, a case the section's opening sends to the first settle prompt.
- **`escalation-rules` §0 sends a picker's owner to *Closed-vocabulary pickers must normalise the free-text answer*.** It said "see the next section", which is *Choice lists are presented verbatim*.
- **`next-phase-offer`'s `/epics` precondition** says "the `<merge-clause>` rules exist to prevent", where it pointed at the rules "below", which stand above it.
- **`session-hygiene` §1 names `resume.md`** as the file whose ownership a resume pointer written by cost emission would split, where it said "this file".
- **`next-phase-offer` names the family and the section it means** where it says when `<merge-clause>` is not attached: the rule is stated against the gate the offer names rather than against membership of the `/product-workflows:brd-*` family, whose glob `/product-workflows:brd-proposal` matches, where it said "the family"; and `/workflows-core:frames`' one offer is `phase-handoff` §4.3's consent choice, where it said "its §4.3".

### Added — the repository's gates (not shipped in any plugin)
- **`scripts/reference-parts.py`**, a gate of its own beside `check-id-grammar.sh`, never a check in `check-docs.sh`, whose body the internal editions share. `generate` assembles the parts and deletes any file under `reference-parts/` that no declaration produces. `check-parts` fails on a malformed declaration or marker, a line outside every region, a rule name in two parts and a generated file that is stale, missing or produced by no declaration, and prints a positional pointer inside a part as a `FLAG`. `check-citations` fails when the loader's list of split references is not the declared set, when a dependent plugin's command, agent or reference cites one without the load-by-part paragraph, on a part request naming no requestable part, on an entry-point request — `args: "<ref> <token>"` on a reference that does not load by part — whose token is not the first word of a code span in one of that reference's headings, the request the loader fails, printing a census of those requests, on a request whose sentence names a rule its load does not hold — a call converted to the wrong part — on a sentence naming a rule of a split reference its paragraph cites, in any form, that no load of it at or before that sentence holds, and on a part file read by path from a dependent plugin, whose own plugin root holds no `reference-parts/`, naming the loader call to make instead — placing every other citation on a part or counting it whole. Both fail closed. `--selftest` runs one fixture tree per case under `scripts/fixtures/reference-parts/`, and CI runs the self-test, `check-parts` and `check-citations` after the docs gate.
- **`.claude/rules/gates-reference-parts.md`** states what the gate enforces and what it cannot see — whether a run loads a part when it reaches the citation, and the part an explicit request takes where its sentence names no rule — and `docs/maintainers/rationale.md` § reference-parts why it generates files, why it fails closed and why it is a script of its own. `CLAUDE.md`'s reference convention describes the part request and the load-by-part paragraph, `gates.md` § Check 16 says a second token is an entry point or, on a split reference, a part, and `.claude/rules/workflows-core.md` names `reference-parts/` and what the loader reads.

### Changed — the repository's instruction files (not shipped in any plugin)
- **`.claude/rules/workflows-core-triage.md`'s recipe** for `finding-triage`'s consumers is `grep -lE 'workflows-core:finding-triage|args: "finding-triage|reference-parts/finding-triage'`, since the bare name now matches every file that carries this release's load-by-part paragraph, and the file says the three reviewer agents state that their caller triages without citing the reference.

### Fixed — the repository's instruction files (not shipped in any plugin)
- **`.claude/rules/docs-workflows.md` counts twenty-two markdown pages** under `docs-workflows`' `references/`, with the command that counts them, where it said twenty-one.

## [1.43.0] — 2026-10-19

**Update `dev-workflows` to 4.29.0 with this release**: every rule change in this release but one is `/dev-workflows:implement`'s alone, a carve-out from a rule that still holds for every other command, and an older `/implement`, which codes inline on the session model and reviews only from `SIGNIFICANT` up, contradicts each. The one is `finding-triage`'s: a `BLOCK` that leaves no `BLOCKER` and no survivor the caller's fixer takes is settled at the first settle prompt, with no fixer dispatched, by every command that triages a review — which each reads from the reference whatever its version, so no other plugin needs updating with it. `escalation-rules`' unspent re-review also now counts a verdict settled as not `BLOCK` at the first settle prompt's **Proceed**, as its offer section already counted one; that changes what no other command does, since each that edits after such a **Proceed** — `/prd-proposal`, `/brd-proposal`, `/epics` — already offers it there, its own text taking the **Proceed** as a verdict that is not `BLOCK`.

### Changed
- **`model-routing/classification` routes `/implement`'s coding by class, and has it review `MODERATE` work.**
  - **§4's coding-model rule.** `/implement` authors no code itself: it dispatches `dev-workflows:code-writer` for each group of plan steps and each Phase 3.5 fix attempt, and its `implementation_model` is the model that dispatch is passed — `current_model` at `SIMPLE`; from `MODERATE` up, `current_model` where it is an Opus or Fable model (a `claude-opus-*` or `claude-fable-*` id), else the §2 Opus chain's first available row; `--enforce-model` over both — re-resolved at a re-classification for every later dispatch. `fixes_model` equals it for `/implement`, so its `review-fixer` follows the same rule, and equals `detection_model` elsewhere; `review_model` is set for `/implement`'s `MODERATE` runs; and another command's `implementation_model` is its delegated writer's or executor's model. `code-writer` joins the agents that receive the `model_routing` block — `classification`, `implementation_model`, and `enforced_model` with `routing` where §10 sets them — acting on none of them, and loading the reference itself only for §1.1's items.
  - **§3.1's carve-out.** `/implement` alone reviews `MODERATE` work with the Opus `code-review` gate, before it runs any test against the working tree, as at `SIGNIFICANT` (§3.2 step 4); every other caller skips the review at `MODERATE` as before, and `SIMPLE` work stays unreviewed. "Continue with the currently selected model" and "Do not add mandatory Opus steps" each name `/implement`'s `code-writer` and `review-fixer` dispatches, on `implementation_model`, as their exception, the second its `MODERATE` gate too.
  - **The rest of the reference states the same exception**, so no two instructions disagree: §3.2 steps 3 and 6 — step 6's rule now stated before its exception — and the note closing §3; §5's post-implementation review; §6's checklist, which the `MODERATE` review covers; §7's report, whose review line reads *n/a — SIMPLE* on `/implement` and whose verdict list a `MODERATE` `/implement` run may not omit; §9.1's mechanical-step principle and §9.2's role map, in a row of its own; and §10, where `/implement`'s `implementation_model` names a dispatched step and so takes the enforced id — it was listed as inline coding, keeping the session model — and a `SIMPLE` or `MODERATE` task still skips the plan and review gates save the one §3.1 gives `/implement`'s `MODERATE` work.
  - **Two wordings corrected.** §1's *When in doubt, escalate one level* says misclassifying upward costs the Opus steps the higher class adds, not one Opus call; and §9's opening says §9.1's principle and §9.2's map govern every command and §9.4 applies them to scanning, where it named §9.4 the governing rule — §9.4 now opens *Scanning is routed by step nature*.
- **`model-routing/classification` names `/implement`'s session-model gate.** §9.1 says a command can gate on `current_model` on every class, before it classifies, where what a session below Opus gets wrong is the orchestration itself, as `/implement` does in its Phase 0 — asking a session that is no Opus or Fable model to stop and relaunch on Opus or to go on anyway, recorded in its report, with no relaunch where no Opus model is available (§9.3) — and that such a gate, which offers going on, is stricter by the command's own choice and no breach of §3.1; §9.2's coordination row names it beside the window advisory for delegated writing; and §10's *Opus-session gates do not fire* lists it among its examples, the orchestration joining the grill and the authoring as what such a gate protects.
- **`finding-triage` states each fixer's tier.** `doc-fixer`, and `review-fixer` on every caller but `/implement`, run on the detection (Sonnet) chain; `/implement`'s `review-fixer` runs on its `implementation_model`, and the triage stays the orchestrator's there too — one rule for every fixer.
- **`finding-triage` settles a `BLOCK` that leaves its fixer nothing to take.** § When triage empties the survivor set now treats as its own case a `BLOCK` whose survivors hold no `BLOCKER` and none the caller's fixer takes, whatever keeps each from it — a grade it does not take, such as a `NIT`, or the caller's own rule, as for a finding whose fix lies in another code repository: no fixer is dispatched and no re-review run on that account, the first settle prompt is asked, reporting every survivor, and its **Proceed** goes on as from a verdict that is not `BLOCK`; a `PASS WITH RECOMMENDATIONS` so left continues without a fixer. It reaches every command that triages a review. Before, only `/implement`'s other-repository case took it, phrased as `BLOCKER` and `MAJOR` left for the fixer and `MINOR` and `NIT` not, and every other such `BLOCK` dispatched a fixer that applied nothing and then re-reviewed.
- **`/implement` makes the offer to apply what a review leaves open.** `escalation-rules`' *Findings a review leaves open — the offer to apply them* is used by `/dev-workflows:implement` too, after its first review and its one re-review and never after its review of the Phase 3.5 delta: its *Taken* bullet says the edits there are `review-fixer`'s, since that orchestrator edits no source — after the first review in that review's one fixer pass, after the re-review in one pass more — and *It is not a fix cycle and raises no cap* says that pass is no second fix cycle, since no review follows it. *The unspent re-review* puts `/implement`'s findings to the offer beside the inline-fixing commands', and `finding-triage` § On re-review rule 1's one exception now reaches a survivor `/implement`'s `review-fixer` applies there. The references page says so.
- **The unspent re-review counts a verdict a settle prompt settled.** `escalation-rules`' *Edits after a verdict that is not BLOCK — the unspent re-review* is offered where the verdict was not `BLOCK`, or was settled as not `BLOCK` at a triage settle prompt's **Proceed**, as the offer section above it already counts one, so `/implement`'s fixer pass for the `NIT`s left open by a `BLOCK` its first settle prompt settled is followed by the re-review offer, as on `PASS WITH RECOMMENDATIONS`. A `BLOCK`'s fix cycle still spends the re-review, and so does a re-review the first settle prompt asked for; a `BLOCK` the first settle prompt's **Proceed** settled before any fixer ran spends neither half, which *It never raises the cap* now says.
- **`finding-triage` says what its first settle prompt's Proceed does where findings survive.** In the `BLOCK` case whose survivors the caller's fixer does not take, **Proceed**, whose label reads *as if the verdict were PASS*, goes on as from a verdict that is not `BLOCK`, the survivors standing and, in a caller that makes one, the offer to apply what a review leaves open made over them; the caller says so beside the prompt, whose array is unchanged.
- **`escalation-rules`' *Closed-vocabulary pickers* names an eleventh**: `/dev-workflows:implement`'s plan-conflict ruling picker (Phase 3B step 7's review-fixer sub-step) — the finding governs and the plan is revised to it, or the fix is applied and the plan text kept, the deviation logged; never a third — so a free-text answer there is normalised or re-asked. "Ten" reads "eleven" at the section's three count sites, and rows 6, 7 and 11 are the three spent inside their run.
- **`session-hygiene` §3 says `/implement` suggests no `/compact` mid-run**, where it placed one at a Scope-to-N checkpoint the command no longer has: its step groups are dispatched back to back, with no user turn between them to act on one, its run-state file carries the run across an automatic compaction, and a step list past one run's reach is decomposed into a unit of its own (`context-management.md`'s *Decompose*). The *Surface* paragraph says it places none, the reference's opening no longer speaks of a mid-phase checkpoint, which no command has, and §3 is retitled *Mid-run suggestions & non-pipeline big commands*.

### Fixed
- **`security-register-format` §3 names a `history` entry's fields and their order**: `{status, at, by, reason, evidence, role, run, links}`, in replay order, the last entry the decision the finding's status stands on, the decider's `by` among them, which `/dev-workflows:implement` reads to name who decided a finding it names outside its task. `register.py` has always written them; the format said only *every decision, with its role and the run that made it*.

## [1.42.0] — 2026-10-17

**Update `dev-workflows` to 4.28.0 with this release**: `next-phase-offer` now offers an Epic's `/dev-workflows:conformance` before the Epic's pull request merges, which an older `/conformance` refuses with `CONFORMANCE_NOT_MERGED`, and both of 4.28.0's `/implement` and `/conformance` load this release's `unit-branch`. The scan it offers is `security-workflows` 0.1.0's, a new plugin that needs this release.

### Added
- **`security-register-format`**, the single source of truth for the findings register `/security-workflows:scan` keeps per code repository in `$SPECS_PATH/security/<repo-slug>/`: where it lives, and why it is bookkeeping that a run's terminal commit carries and no pull request hands off; the run record, written once and replayed in `(seq, at, file name)` order into the derived `register.json` and `register.md`; numbering and renumbering, a finding being named by its permanent `origin`, never by its number, and a published number staying where it was published — a collision moves only the finding whose record sorts later, onto the number another of its own allocations was last published under where that number is its own, and otherwise to the lowest number above it nobody published, never back — with a move that a run recording nothing reports marked provisional; a finding's fields, and the rule that a follow-up or a prompt built from one uses structured fields only, never its text; the statuses and the two roles, a Dev never blocked, and a fix nobody queued — a `fix` finding, or a `fixing` one no line in the unit's `follow-ups.md` carries under a number held for it or as its own line, as its run wrote it — offered again, unless the scan closes it or proposes it fixed, a finding the run reopens never starting from the patch recorded before the reopen; and the rules that move a finding between statuses — matching by Claude Security's own finding ID, closure on evidence only and never by a change scan, a squash merge — or, for a finding raised for no unit and last seen off the default branch, which no subject can name, any later commit of the default branch outside its sighting's base, whose proposal is marked so that its row suggests deciding later — that only proposes a closure, a changed `ID_VERSION` judged per finding, ancestry rather than dates, judged finding by finding, a scan's `scan_prefix` read so a subdirectory scan's paths are repository-relative, a file gone proposed fixed only at a scan later than the finding's last sighting, the same later test choosing a `--verify-fixes` scope, which names as unreachable a finding whose last sighting the default branch's head is not later than, and idempotence; and that a stale copy of the derived files is harmless while a conflicted one stops a rebase, which `register.py rebuild` settles from the run records, each a file of its own. It lives here because a reference does not cross a plugin boundary; `security-workflows`' `register.py` implements it.
- **`implementation-format` §5, `unit-branch`**: which branch carries a unit's work in one repository, and what state it is in — `none`, `merged`, `checked-out`, `local`, `remote` or `missing`, with `after_merge` for work added to the branch after its pull request merged. `/dev-workflows:implement` continues on that branch, `/dev-workflows:conformance` traces it and `/security-workflows:scan` scans it. It reads the latest block naming the repository, never the newest block overall.
  - **The merged test moves here** from `/dev-workflows:conformance`, with its substance intact: the recorded commit an ancestor of the default branch, or a default-branch commit since the block whose subject carries `[<key>]` as a whole token.
  - **A content test beside it.** `git merge-tree --write-tree` asks whether the branch's tip adds anything to the merged commit or to the default branch, so a branch that went on after its pull request merged reads `after_merge`, and — a third leg — a squash whose subject lost `[<key>]` still reads `merged` where the branch survives and its tip adds nothing to the default branch. The test has three outcomes, and a call that compared nothing never reads as "adds work".
  - **What it cannot see is stated.** On a read-only mount each call runs with a scratch object directory, the clone's own store its alternate, so that store gains no object; that block runs in one Bash call, as a subshell that ends `rc=$?; rm -rf "$d"; exit $rc`, so its outcome is the merge's own exit status, never the cleanup's, and whatever follows it in the call, a second block included, still runs. In a shallow clone, whose content test compares nothing, and on a git older than 2.38, which has no `merge-tree --write-tree`, the merged test decides alone and the consumer says it did not compare the tip; a shallow clone can read a merged branch as open, and the consumer says that too.
  - **Its refs are local, so a consumer fetches first**, the block's branch included by its explicit refspec, `+<branch>:refs/remotes/origin/<branch>`, since a single-branch clone's plain fetch never creates `origin/<branch>`. Where that fetch found the branch not on `origin` and its tracking ref still resolves, the one stale ref is removed, on a writable clone, as `--prune` would; a stale local branch left behind whose squash lost `[<key>]` then reads as open, as the branch of a pull request under review would.
- **Two bookkeeping shapes in `specs-repo-git` §2.1** — ten shapes now, five directories and five single files: `security/<repo-slug>/`, a register with a keyless scan's cost entries beside it, and a unit's `_security.md`. The first names the register's own paths only — `runs/`, `scans/`, `dev-workflows/`, `register.json` and `register.md` in each `security/<repo-slug>/` — so a team's own document under `security/`, a threat model or a policy, is never swept into a prompt-free session commit. `commit-artifacts` commits and pushes them prompt-free and never hands them off by pull request. `session-branch.py`'s classifier stages both, its merge attributes append `_security.md` and keep this run's `register.json` and `register.md` whole, and its self-test covers each.
- **Cost for `/scan`.** A `cost-emission` §7 row whose phase is `security-audit` and whose role comes from the `run_role` the command passes — `dev` or `sec`, and `n/a` where it is missing. A scan with no key files its entry per code repository, under `security/<repo-slug>/dev-workflows/cost/`, a new branch of §8's tier 2 ahead of pending, and only reports it where no slug resolved: never the pending file, which such an entry would never leave. A correction by `/feedback`, `/prompt`, `/prompt-brainstorm` or `/prompt-grill-me` whose target is `/scan` inherits its phase and records the role as `n/a`.
- **`session-cost.py` counts a `Workflow` run's agents**, which write their transcripts one level down, to `subagents/workflows/<run-id>/agent-*.jsonl` — the run's `journal.jsonl` beside them is not counted — so a Claude Security scan's agents are in its cost. `cost-emission` §1, `run-flags`' `skip-cost` and `session-diagnosis` name the directory too.
- **`command-namespaces.json` lists `security-workflows` and its `scan`**, so a cost boundary is drawn at a scan.

### Changed
- **The Dev's offers come before the merge.** `next-phase-offer` routes `/dev-workflows:implement <EPIC>` to `/dev-workflows:conformance <EPIC>` and then `/security-workflows:scan <EPIC>`, both before the Epic's pull request merges, and the fixes from either back to `/implement`, which continues on the Epic's branch. An Epic run of `/conformance` with nothing to fix offers the scan from each repository whose branch it traced before the merge, and a new line routes the scan itself: a fix to `/implement`, `--verify-fixes` once it has merged, and with none the pull request's review and merge — where the scanned branch went on after its pull request merged (`after_merge`), the review and merge of the pull request that `/implement` run opens, and none where nothing went to fix — and either way the scan from each other repository the Epic's `implementation.md` names, and `--triage` where accepted risks are past their date or decisions await security review. **This reverses 1.40.1**, which offered an Epic's `/conformance` once its pull request had merged. The role labels gain the Security Engineer, every offer of the key scan names the Dev, whose `dev` run it is, and the scan's offers carry no `<merge-clause>`, since nothing it writes is gated.
- **The family map draws `security-workflows`**: the Dev's scan in the Dev lane after the Epic's `/conformance`, which is now drawn before the pull request merges, and a Security Engineer lane for a repository scan, `--verify-fixes` and `--triage`.
- **The family is five plugins.** `dependencies` says what `security-workflows` requires and why the marketplace allowlists `claude-plugins-official`, and that a dependency from another marketplace is declared `<name>@<marketplace>`, with no version pin. `feedback-emission`'s plugin-facing predicate, `bounded-run`, `escalation-rules`' missing-path rule, `followup-emission` (twelve callers), `session-hygiene`'s role labels and the docs pages name the new plugin, and the roles-and-phases page describes `security-audit` and says the lifecycle phases are read off §7's table and the inference paragraphs beside it, which alone name `conformance` and `security-audit`.
- **`followup-emission`'s *Re-queuing a confirmed fix* says when `/dev-workflows:conformance` does not re-queue**: a line that a `conformance/` branch carrying the Epic's record holds open is not re-queued, since it arrives with that branch — new in dev-workflows 4.28.0. The section now also states two cases that command already re-queued before this release, and still does: a line equal to a `withdraws:` value of the PRD run's own record, and a line only a session branch carries, which the PRD run cannot see, the two standing side by side once that branch merges.
- **`followup-emission` §5 matches a line carrying a `[SEC#n]` only against an open line carrying the same `[SEC#n]` for the same repository and unit**, never against a ticked line or a line without that token, so a finding `/security-workflows:scan` reopens after its fix landed is queued again beside its old ticked line, and a line another caller wrote for the same change in its own words does not stand for it. A re-run still writes it once.

### Fixed
- **`/feedback` and `/prompt` no longer say a keyless run lands in the pending file "exactly as `/idea`'s does"**: `/idea` always resolves a folder and never reaches it. Each now says the run lands there as §8 tier 2's *Otherwise* sends it, and `cost-emission` §9 names the runs that reach it the same way, `/diagnose-session` among them.
- **`test-notify` notifies a Gradle run issued as `cleanTest test`**, the command `dev-workflows` 4.28.0's `test-baseliner` now runs in capture and verify alike, `./gradlew cleanTest test --no-build-cache`: its substring matcher found neither `gradlew test` nor `gradle test` in it, so it gains `gradlew cleanTest test` and `gradle cleanTest test`, which match it, and the hooks page lists them.
- **`specs-repo-git`'s G2 notice and §4.1 say where a direct `/dev-workflows:implement` run from inside the specs repository commits its session files when it stops before it cuts its branch**: on the branch HEAD then stands on, which is the default branch where `dev-workflows` 4.28.0's Phase 1.4 answered a fresh start and switched to it before the stop, and which `commit-artifacts` then pushes. The notice said they stayed on the branch the run started on, unpushed, which held only while the switch came straight before the branch was cut.
- **`specs-repo-git` §8.3 names `/dev-workflows:implement`'s Phase 1.4** among the moves its lift and put-back surround on a direct run from inside the specs repository: the clean-tree check that lifts the session files, and a fresh start's switch, moved there from Pre-Phase 3 in `dev-workflows` 4.28.0.

### Changed — the repository's gates (not shipped in any plugin)
- **`scripts/check-docs.sh` gates `security-workflows`**, which `PLUGIN_RELS` and `COST_PLUGIN_RELS` now list, **and check 9 accepts a singular count**, `one slash command` and `one command emits a cost entry`, which a plugin of one command needs. Three selftest cases: two reword the fixture into the singular and must stay green, and one claims two commands as one and must fail. The change is to the body shared with the internal editions, and `docs/superpowers/to-fix.md` lists porting it as E3.
- **CI runs `register.py --selftest`.**
- **`scripts/check-id-grammar.sh` rejects a dash form of `SEC`**, `[SEC-1]` and a bare `SEC-2`, beside the PRD and ARD prefixes: both alternations gain `SEC`, and its selftest's fixtures carry each form. It fires on no line of the tree.
- **`docs/superpowers/to-fix.md`** drops the `/conformance` items C1 and C3–C8, fixed in this release and dev-workflows 4.28.0 or moot, and keeps C2, with a new C9, under the merged test, which now lives in §5; C2 now names what `/security-workflows:scan` does on the same misread, so the §5 fix is checked against all three `unit-branch` consumers.

## [1.41.2] — 2026-10-15

### Fixed
- **`ard-resolution` reads an ARD that only the default branch held once row C's repair has brought it into the working tree.** 1.41.1 said any ref-only ARD the gate passed was not used, which would have dropped exactly that one; only row B — the run's own branch deleted the file — leaves nothing to read. The new `ls-tree` listing is quiet, and adds no candidate, where `$SPECS_PATH` is no repository or the default ref does not exist.
- **Row B′'s conflict advice keeps every line either side holds**, a line one side changed in place in its changed form and a heading both sides wrote once. 1.41.1's "keep both sides" of `follow-ups.md` left a follow-up `/dev-workflows:conformance` had ticked beside its open twin.

## [1.41.1] — 2026-10-15

### Fixed
- **`ard-resolution` brings an ARD that reached the default branch after the run's own branch was cut to `require-on-main`**, which stops on row B′. Step 2 collected candidate ARD files from the working tree alone, so a `/specify`, `/design` or `/ready` run resuming on a branch cut before the ARD merged resolved `status: none` and skipped ARD conformance without a word — the case 1.41.0's "missing from the branch's working tree, stops the same way" claimed and did not reach for the ARD. Step 2 now lists each folder on `<default-ref>` too (`git ls-tree --name-only`); a candidate only the ref holds goes through the gate like any other.
- **Row B′'s stop says how to settle the merge it asks for outside session-branch mode.** There the branch carries the run's session files, and `dev-workflows/resume.md`, which every run rewrites, has commonly changed on both sides, so `git merge <default-ref>` stopped on a conflict the message never mentioned — and a re-run on the unfinished merge could not commit. The plain form now says to keep both sides of an appended session file and either side of `resume.md`, then commit the merge before re-running.

## [1.41.0] — 2026-10-13

### Changed
- **`require-on-main` stops a run resuming on its own branch where a gated document changed on the default branch after that branch was cut** — `phase-handoff` §3.3's new row B′, ahead of row B. Row B passed every branch of the run's own whose copy differed from the default branch, so `/dev-workflows:ready`, `/dev-workflows:conformance`, or a resumed `/dev-workflows:design` that had amended nothing yet read the stale copy the branch was cut with, silently. B′ tests the worktree against the fork point (`git merge-base HEAD <default-ref>`): where the branch changed the document — committed, staged, unstaged, or as an untracked copy at the path — row B passes exactly as before, `/design`'s amended `specification.md` included; where it did not, the run stops and names `git -C "$SPECS_PATH" merge <default-ref>`, which the operator runs. A document that reached the default branch after the cut, missing from the branch's working tree, stops the same way. In session-branch mode the stop names `session-branch.py lift` first, since git refuses that merge over the session files the overlay holds. `ard-resolution` names B′ among the stopping states that stop the caller as `unmerged` does, §3.4's BRD-route register row and its note on rows D/E name it among the register's stops, and `specs-repo-git` §3.5 and §3.6 say which of B and B′ a resumed run meets.

## [1.40.1] — 2026-10-13

### Changed
- **`next-phase-offer` offers an Epic's `/dev-workflows:conformance` once the Epic's code pull request has merged**, not while it is open, and a fix's re-check once that fix has merged (dev-workflows 4.26.0, which checks an Epic's merged code only). The family map draws the Dev's Epic run the same way.
- **`followup-emission`'s *Withdrawing a follow-up* says where `/dev-workflows:conformance` ticks a line**: wherever the checkout's copy holds it. A line the checkout does not hold yet — on a Dev's unmerged `conformance/` branch, or on a session branch — is named in the run's report and ticked by a later PRD run once the checkout holds it; until then the line the PRD record carries as `withdraws:` keeps `/dev-workflows:implement` from building it (dev-workflows 4.26.0).
- **`phase-handoff` §4.0's `_conformance.md` row names `/dev-workflows:implement` among its readers**, for a PRD record's `withdraws:` lines (dev-workflows 4.26.0), and says what a later run does with a handed-off record: `/dev-workflows:conformance` now stops while a `conformance/` branch carries the unit's record unmerged, so the row no longer says no run gates on it — and says why the record stays advisory: a declined handoff leaves it in the working copy, which the next run reads.

## [1.40.0] — 2026-10-13

### Changed
- **A selection carries no text** — a new rule in `escalation-rules` §0. Choosing an option returns its label and nothing else, so an option that needs the operator's text (a name, a path, a key, a reason, a correction) is labelled `… (you'll be prompted)` or `… (you'll be prompted for <what>)` whatever the rest of its wording, and is followed at once by one plain-text question for it, and the run never reads the bare selection as a refusal or a broken promise; a free-text answer that already carries the text is taken as given, and a question whose whole answer is text with no default is asked in plain text, never as a picker. The shared arrays follow it: *Required path environment variable unset* offers `Set <VAR> (you'll be prompted for the path)` and says what the answer becomes, *key dir not found* offers `Re-enter key (you'll be prompted)`, the repo-escalation and branch-prefix arrays are relabelled, and the branch-prefix identity variant asks for initials in one plain-text prompt with no picker — the convention named in its opening line, a `cancel` (or blank) answer ending the run — in both `escalation-rules` and `branch-naming` §2.5. The BLOCK escalations' *Override and accept the finding* reads `(you'll be prompted for the rationale)` and says the rationale is asked in plain text; `architecture-promotion`'s *Change it* is relabelled the same way. `epic-picker`'s remainder option reads `Another Epic from the list above (you'll be prompted for its key)`.
- **The REPOS_PATH question is gone where the path resolves** (dev-workflows 4.25.0, product-workflows 3.34.0, docs-workflows 1.16.0): *Required path environment variable unset* now lists `/design`, `/specify`, `/create-ard`, `/epics`, `/document` and `/release-notes` among its `REPOS_PATH` citers, which ask only where no entry of `${REPOS_PATH:-/workspace}` is an existing directory and otherwise print a `Repos:` line. A citer whose run can go on without code offers that way on beside *Set REPOS_PATH* and *Cancel*; only `REPOS_PATH`'s reason for the stop is narrowed, `SPECS_PATH` staying a stop.
- **`grilling-technique` recommends an answer only where one exists**: a fact only the operator holds, with no default — a company name, a person, a URL — is asked in plain text with no recommendation and no options. `/prompt-grill-me`, `/design`, `/idea`, `/create-prd`, `/update-prd`, `/create-ard` and `/specify` say the same where they restate the technique.

## [1.39.3] — 2026-10-13

### Fixed
- **`epic-picker` describes the commands that use its picker rules without making the Epic pick, rather than listing them.** The list named four documentation commands and had missed `/product-workflows:brd-reconcile` and, since 1.39.2, `/dev-workflows:conformance`, which picks the Epic that carries a fix; a description cannot go stale as members join, and the `grep` beside it still re-derives the set.

## [1.39.2] — 2026-10-13

### Fixed
- **`next-phase-offer`'s routing graph draws `/dev-workflows:conformance`.** Since dev-workflows 4.24.0 `/implement` offers it after an Epic and the PE runs it on the PRD before `/document`, but the graph went from `/implement` straight to `/document`. It now names the Dev's Epic run, while the Epic's pull request is open, the PE's PRD run once every Epic has merged, and each run's own next steps; the `<merge-clause>` rule names `/implement` and `/conformance` among its adopters.
- **`specs-repo-git` reads a `conformance/` branch as a plugin branch everywhere.** 1.37.0 added the prefix to §2.1 and row G2 but not to `branch-key`'s strip list, so a key was never read off a `conformance/` branch, nor to the G2 notice's list of plugin prefixes.
- **`followup-emission` states what `/conformance` does with a follow-up.** §2 and §8 let a caller whose items belong to different units name `unit_folder` and `key` per item — a PRD run queues for several Epics at once — and § *Withdrawing a follow-up* covers a PRD run's own lines on a re-run, and a withdrawal on a requirement withdrawn or gone on any run, not only the PE's overrule of a Dev's.
- **`cost-emission` labels a `/conformance` refusal on a BRD container** `conformance`/`pe`. The refusal writes its cost entry under the container's key, and the inference said there was no such case.
- **`grounding-format` §4 names a clone with no readable `origin` by its directory's basename however its path arrived** — typed, given to `--repos`, or passed by `/brd-split`'s *Ground now* — so a later typed re-run records the same name in `baselines.md` rather than a second repository (product-workflows 3.33.3). A clone with an `origin` is still named by its slug, never by a directory.
- **`escalation-rules`' missing-path rule names `/brd-split`'s *Ground now* as a reader of `REPOS_PATH`.**
- **`specs-repo-git` §4 and `cost-emission` name the one stop that takes no refusal tail**: `/prd-ground`'s `PRD_GROUND_TYPED_ONLY`, where the model started it without *Ground now* — no run started, so there is no cost entry to write (product-workflows 3.33.3).
- **`/workflows-core:statusline`'s report says the snapshot is enabled given `jq` on the `PATH` the status line runs with** — the one Claude Code started with — rather than the one its own check ran in, which loads your profile and can differ.

### Upgrading from 1.39.0 in session-branch mode
- A `release-notes.md` change your session branch carried from before 1.39.0 keeps being reported after that branch's pull request merges: the draft the overlay placed stays modified against your local `HEAD`, which no step returns to it any more, so every preflight skips its catch-up. Once that pull request has merged, check that `git -C "$SPECS_PATH" diff --quiet origin/<default> -- <PRD folder>/release-notes.md` succeeds — the working copy holds nothing the merge did not land — then run `git -C "$SPECS_PATH" restore -- <PRD folder>/release-notes.md`; the next run catches up and stops reporting it.
